With <sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>e-level outcome rewards, GRPO assi<sub>g</sub>ns one res<sub>p</sub>onse advanta<sub>g</sub>e to all <sub>g</sub>enerated tokens (Shao et al., 2024). A shared res<sub>p</sub>onse coeficient does not <sub>p</sub>reserve these element-s<sub>p</sub>ecific contrasts. O<sub>ur con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>ons are</sub> f<sub>our</sub>f<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>:</sub>

• Reference-element credit assignment. We retain individual element identities across full-page rollouts <sub>an</sub>d <sub>c</sub>h<sub>arac</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> th<sub>e</sub> l<sub>oca</sub>l <sub>qua</sub>lit<sub>y con</sub>t<sub>ras</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>aggrega</sub>ti<sub>on can erase un</sub>d<sub>er a</sub> fi<sub>xe</sub>d<sub>-we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht <sub>a</sub>dditi<sub>ve page</sub> <sub>u</sub>tilit<sub>y.</sub>

• Complete outcome feedback. We propose the UAP, which outlines a unified set of rules for handling <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>es, manag</sub>i<sub>ng m</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>ng va</sub>l<sub>ues, an</sub>d <sub>a</sub>dd<sub>ress</sub>i<sub>ng un</sub>if<sub>orm</sub>l<sub>y</sub> i<sub>mper</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>comes.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>es a</sub> f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>mec</sub>h<sub>an</sub>i<sub>sm</sub> f<sub>or</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ances w</sub>h<sub>ere an en</sub>ti<sub>re e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t i<sub>s a</sub>b<sub>sen</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

• Budget-preserving intra-block redistribution. • Empirical efectiveness and generality. Page-to-Block Policy Optimization (P2BPO) normalizes each matched reference group independently:

$$

Unified Anchor Pricing (UAP) completes each reference element’s outcomes across valid rollouts $\mathcal { V } _ { k } \colon Q _ { i , k } = s _ { i , k }$ <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>an</sub>d $Q _ { i , k } = 0$ <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en m</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub> With <sub>mean</sub> $\mu _ { k }$ <sub>an</sub>d <sub>popu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d d<sub>ev</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> $\sigma _ { k } ,$ it<sub>s coe</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t <sub>uses re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve compar</sub>i<sub>son w</sub>h<sub>en ou</sub>t<sub>comes</sub> dif<sub>er an</sub>d <sub>ce</sub>ili<sub>ng-gap</sub> f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> di<sub>spers</sub>i<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s neg</sub>li<sub>g</sub>ibl<sub>e:</sub>

$$
\begin{array} { r } { A _ { i , k } ^ { \mathrm { U } } = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { ( Q _ { i , k } - \mu _ { k } ) / \sigma _ { k } , } & { \sigma _ { k } > \epsilon , } \\ { - \lambda h ( \mu _ { k } , c _ { k } ) , } & { \sigma _ { k } \leq \epsilon , } \end{array} \right. \qquad h ( u , c ) = \operatorname* { m a x } ( 0 , c - u ) . } \end{array}\tag{2}
$$

Here $\lambda \geq 0$ <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e ce</sub>ili<sub>ng gap;</sub> th<sub>e same</sub> t<sub>o</sub>l<sub>erance � separa</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> b<sub>ranc</sub>h<sub>es.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> $\lambda > 0 _ { ; }$ <sub>,</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>mper</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t ti<sub>es an</sub>d <sub>a</sub>ll<sub>-m</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>ng groups rece</sub>i<sub>ve nega</sub>ti<sub>ve coe</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s, w</sub>h<sub>ereas a ce</sub>ili<sub>ng-qua</sub>lit<sub>y</sub> ti<sub>e rece</sub>i<sub>ves</sub> <sub>zero.</sub> Th<sub>us a s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>-</sub>i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>exe</sub>d <sub>ru</sub>l<sub>e re</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k f<sub>or m</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>ng an</sub>d <sub>un</sub>if<sub>orm</sub>l<sub>y</sub> i<sub>mper</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>comes.</sub>

## 2.3. SCR: redistribute credit under a conserved budget

Span Credit Redistribution (SCR) reallocates an available block coeficient under a conserved budget, concent<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>ng nega</sub>ti<sub>ve cre</sub>dit i<sub>n error reg</sub>i<sub>ons an</sub>d <sub>pos</sub>iti<sub>ve cre</sub>dit i<sub>n correc</sub>t <sub>reg</sub>i<sub>ons.</sub>

Quality gating restricts redistribution to high-scoring, type-matched blocks, focusing local allocation on <sub>res</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>s w</sub>ithi<sub>n o</sub>th<sub>erw</sub>i<sub>se accura</sub>t<sub>e e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> F<sub>or e</sub>li<sub>g</sub>ibl<sub>e ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>s,</sub> SCR <sub>crea</sub>t<sub>es e</sub>dit<sub>-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>mas</sub>k<sub>s</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>on</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th th<sub>e pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d th<sub>e re</sub>f<sub>erence.</sub> I<sub>nser</sub>ti<sub>ons an</sub>d <sub>rep</sub>l<sub>acemen</sub>t<sub>s</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tif<sub>y error</sub> l<sub>oca</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> SCR <sub>ass</sub>i<sub>gns</sub> d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>e</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>re</sub>f<sub>erence con</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n a ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d bl<sub>oc</sub>k t<sub>o a near</sub>b<sub>y genera</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>pos</sub>iti<sub>on.</sub> Wh<sub>o</sub>l<sub>e-e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t <sub>om</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>ons</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub> UAP’<sub>s separa</sub>t<sub>e</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>ary rou</sub>t<sub>e.</sub> F<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>s appear</sub> i<sub>n</sub> A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> B<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub> A bi<sub>nary mas</sub>k $u _ { i , t }$ selects errors for ne<sub>g</sub>ative credit and correct <sub>p</sub>ositions for <sub>p</sub>ositive credit (see Fi<sub>g</sub>. 2(b)). For a nonem<sub>p</sub>t<sub>y</sub> mask <sub>o</sub>f <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> $\begin{array} { r } { N _ { i , k } = \sum _ { t \in \mathcal { B } _ { i , k } } u _ { i , t } } \end{array}$ th<sub>e re</sub>di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> h<sub>as</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>orm</sub>

$$
d _ { i , t } = 1 - \eta _ { i , k } + \eta _ { i , k } \frac { L _ { i , k } u _ { i , t } } { N _ { i , k } } , \qquad \sum _ { t \in \mathcal { B } _ { i , k } } d _ { i , t } = L _ { i , k } .\tag{3}
$$

## 2.4. Page-to-token composition

Let $\mathcal { K } _ { i }$ <sub>an</sub>d $M _ { i }$ i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ex ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>an</sub>d <sub>m</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>ng re</sub>f<sub>erence e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s, respec</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y.</sub> F<sub>or eac</sub>h <sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>, we c</sub>li<sub>p raw</sub> page-reward dimensions to [0, 1] and average over the available dimensions. Normalizing these scalar rewards <sub>across ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>s</sub> f<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e same promp</sub>t <sub>g</sub>i<sub>ves</sub> $A _ { i } ^ { \check { \mathsf { P } } }$ (A<sub>pp</sub>endix B.1). Combinin<sub>g</sub> the two local contributions with <sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>e f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>g</sub>i<sub>ves</sub>

$$
C _ { i , t } ^ { \mathrm { U S } } = \sum _ { k \in \mathcal { K } _ { i } } A _ { i , k } ^ { \mathrm { U } } \mathbf { 1 } [ t \in \mathcal { B } _ { i , k } ] d _ { i , t } + \sum _ { k \in \mathcal { M } _ { i } } A _ { i , k } ^ { \mathrm { U } } \nu _ { i , k } ( t ) ,\tag{4}
$$

$$
A _ { i , t } ^ { \mathrm { U S } } = ( 1 - \omega ) A _ { i } ^ { \mathrm { P } } + \omega C _ { i , t } ^ { \mathrm { U S } } , \qquad \omega \in [ 0 , 1 ] .
$$

## 3. Related Work

Document parsing and reinforcement post-training. End-to-end parsers such as Donut and Nougat generate structured documents directl<sub>y</sub> from ima<sub>g</sub>es (Kim et al., 2022; Blecher et al., 2024). Recent s<sub>y</sub>stems extend this settin<sub>g</sub> to diverse la<sub>y</sub>outs and ca<sub>p</sub>ture conditions (Cai et al., 2026; Lu et al., 2026). Reinforcement <sub>p</sub>ost-trainin<sub>g</sub> introduces rewards for transcri<sub>p</sub>tion, la<sub>y</sub>out, readin<sub>g</sub> order, out<sub>p</sub>ut validit<sub>y</sub>, and structural fidelit<sub>y</sub> (Wan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025<sub>;</sub> Huan<sub>g</sub> et al.<sub>,</sub> 2026<sub>;</sub> Chen et al.<sub>,</sub> 2025<sub>;</sub> Poznanski et al.<sub>,</sub> 2025<sub>;</sub> Wan<sub>g</sub> et al.<sub>,</sub> 2026a<sub>;</sub> Hun<sub>y</sub>uan Vision Team et al., 2025; Zhan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2026). Table- and element-s<sub>p</sub>ecific a<sub>pp</sub>roaches further tailor the <sub>p</sub>rediction unit and reward to local content (Zhan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025; Li et al., 2026a; Wan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2026c). These s<sub>y</sub>stems motivate h<sub>e</sub>t<sub>erogeneous qua</sub>lit<sub>y measures, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> P2TPO <sub>s</sub>t<sub>u</sub>di<sub>es</sub> h<sub>ow</sub> f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k f<sub>or</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>v</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l <sub>re</sub>f<sub>erence e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s reac</sub>h<sub>es</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens w</sub>ithi<sub>n a</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>-page ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>.</sub> A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> A<sub>.</sub>1 <sub>compares rewar</sub>d <sub>un</sub>it<sub>s an</sub>d <sub>op</sub>ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on suppor</sub>t<sub>s.</sub>

Group-relative optimization and reward normalization. GRPO and RLOO construct response advantages from sam<sub>p</sub>led <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s without a learned critic (Shao et al., 2024; Ahmadian et al., 2024). GSPO uses se<sub>q</sub>uencel<sub>eve</sub>l i<sub>mpor</sub>t<sub>ance we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>hti<sub>ng an</sub>d <sub>c</sub>li<sub>pp</sub>i<sub>ng, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> DAPO <sub>an</sub>d D<sub>r.</sub> GRPO <sub>rev</sub>i<sub>se samp</sub>li<sub>ng, c</sub>li<sub>pp</sub>i<sub>ng, re</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>on, or</sub> normalization (Zhen<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025; Yu et al., 2025a; Liu et al., 2025). GDPO, RDPO, and GD<sup>2</sup>PO address the normalization and interaction of multi<sub>p</sub>le reward dimensions (Liu et al., 2026b; Bai et al., 2026; Liu et al., 2026a). NGRPO combines virtual-maximum calibration with as<sub>y</sub>mmetric cli<sub>pp</sub>in<sub>g</sub> to learn from homo<sub>g</sub>eneous failures (Nan et al., 2025). UAP shares ceilin<sub>g</sub>-informed feedback, while unif<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> missin<sub>g</sub> outcomes and i<sub>mper</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>rewar</sub>d <sub>consensus w</sub>ithi<sub>n re</sub>f<sub>erence-e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t <sub>compar</sub>i<sub>sons an</sub>d <sub>rou</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k t<sub>o genera</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens.</sub> A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> C<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub>4 d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>ase</sub>li<sub>nes;</sub> A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> A<sub>.</sub>1 <sub>compares rewar</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>f<sub>aces.</sub>

Structured and local credit assignment. Process su ervision evaluates reasonin ste s (Li htman et al., 2024; Wan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2024); GiGPO com<sub>p</sub>ares re<sub>p</sub>eated environment states while retainin<sub>g</sub> trajector<sub>y</sub> feedback (Fen<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025). CSPO extends GRPO with table-wide functional-attribute rewards whose normalized advanta<sub>g</sub>es are routed to corres<sub>p</sub>ondin<sub>g</sub> tokens alon<sub>g</sub>side <sub>g</sub>lobal feedback (Yan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2026). P2TPO <sub>p</sub>erforms local com<sub>p</sub>arisons f<sub>or</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>v</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l <sub>re</sub>f<sub>erence-</sub>bl<sub>oc</sub>k i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ances</sub> i<sub>n</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>-page ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>s, preserv</sub>i<sub>ng qua</sub>lit<sub>y</sub> dif<sub>erences</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s o</sub>f th<sub>e same</sub> t<sub>ype an</sub>d <sub>suppor</sub>ti<sub>ng om</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>on</sub> f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>an</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>ra-</sub>bl<sub>oc</sub>k <sub>re</sub>di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> F<sub>unc</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>l<sub>-a</sub>tt<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e assessmen</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>re</sub>f<sub>erence-</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ance compar</sub>i<sub>son are comp</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>ary;</sub> A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> A<sub>.</sub>2 di<sub>scusses poss</sub>ibl<sub>e a</sub>d<sub>ap</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub>

## 4.1. Experimental setup

Baselines. We com are P2TPO with four res onse-level olic o timization baselines: GRPO (Shao et al. 2024), GSPO (Zhen<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025), GDPO (Liu et al., 2026b), and RDPO (Bai et al., 2026). P2BPO and its +UAP and +SCR variants serve as com<sub>p</sub>onent ablations. A<sub>pp</sub>endix C.1.4 details the com<sub>p</sub>arison methods.

Benchmarks. We evaluate on six benchmarks with 13 splits: OmniDocBench v1.6 (Ouyang et al., 2025; OmniDocBench contributors, 2026) for hetero<sub>g</sub>eneous full-<sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>arsin<sub>g</sub>; PureDocBench (Li et al., 2026c) for Clean, Di<sub>g</sub>ital, and Real conditions; FinixDocBench (Wan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2026b) for di<sub>g</sub>ital and <sub>p</sub>hoto<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hed documents; Real5-OmniDocBench (Zhou et al., 2026) for five ca<sub>p</sub>ture conditions; DocPTBench (Du et al.,

2025) for <sub>p</sub>hoto<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hed and dewar<sub>p</sub>ed <sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>es; and Wild-OmniDocBench (Li et al., 2026b) for real-world <sub>cap</sub>t<sub>ures.</sub> S<sub>cores compare me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s w</sub>ithi<sub>n eac</sub>h b<sub>enc</sub>h<sub>mar</sub>k<sub>:</sub> fi<sub>ve</sub> b<sub>enc</sub>h<sub>mar</sub>k<sub>s use</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>h<sub>are</sub>d O<sub>mn</sub>iD<sub>oc</sub>B<sub>enc</sub>h <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>or, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> Fi<sub>n</sub>i<sub>x</sub>D<sub>oc</sub>B<sub>enc</sub>h <sub>oo</sub>l<sub>s</sub> it<sub>s</sub> Di it<sub>a</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d Ph<sub>o</sub>t<sub>o</sub> t<sub>rac</sub>k<sub>s us</sub>i<sub>n</sub> it<sub>s o</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>a</sub>l <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>or.</sub> R<sub>ea</sub>l5 <sub>an</sub>d Wild reuse OmniDocBench source <sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>es and assess ca<sub>p</sub>ture robustness. A<sub>pp</sub>endix C.1.5 s<sub>p</sub>ecifies the evaluated sco<sub>p</sub>e <sub>an</sub>d <sub>score</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>n</sub>iti<sub>ons,</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e a</sub>d<sub>ap</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d D<sub>oc</sub>PTB<sub>enc</sub>h <sub>compos</sub>it<sub>e.</sub>

Implementation details. We use converted Infinit -Doc2-5M annotations (Huan et al., 2026) for document SFT followed by RL. SFT provides the initial checkpoint: Qwen3.5-2B trained on 700K pages in the primary setting, with additional settings covering 50K-page checkpoints, Qwen3.5-4B, and Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct. ## 5. Theoretical Analysis

What is lost in aggregation? P2BPO compares individual reference blocks; element-group feedback pools <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>p</sub>l<sub>e</sub> bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>s, suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> th<sub>ose o</sub>f th<sub>e same</sub> t<sub>ype, an</sub>d <sub>page</sub> f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>poo</sub>l<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e groups.</sub> F<sub>or common ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d rollouts, let Q contain scores of � blocks, H center across rollouts, and B = HQ. With fixed positive normalized weights, M pools blocks into � nonempty groups and � combines their scores into an additive page utility:

A<sub>pp</sub>endix D.1 <sub>p</sub>roves this result (Pro<sub>p</sub>osition D.1) and <sub>q</sub>uantifies the coeficient-reconstruction error im<sub>p</sub>osed b<sub>y</sub> coarser routin<sub>g</sub> (Lemma D.2).

Target. end2end: complete heterogeneous page transcription; table, formula, text: recognition of the specified content type. element is reserved for a shared, t<sub>ype-promp</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>reg</sub>i<sub>on recogn</sub>i<sub>zer w</sub>ithi<sub>n a s</sub>t<sub>age</sub>d <sub>page parser; eac</sub>h <sub>ca</sub>ll t<sub>ranscr</sub>ib<sub>es on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>reg</sub>i<sub>on.</sub> It d<sub>oes no</sub>t d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> P2TPO’<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erna</sub>l <sub>re</sub>f<sub>erence e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> A <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e-</sub>t<sub>ype</sub> RL <sub>rec</sub>i<sub>pe</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> it<sub>s</sub> <sub>spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>,</sub> <sub>e.g.,</sub> M<sub>on</sub>k<sub>ey</sub>OCR’<sub>s</sub> t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>rec</sub>i<sub>pe.</sub>  
Optimizer signal. Scalar: one normalized response advantage broadcast over its tokens; a test pass rate still belongs here. Sum/Product → Scalar: aggregate reward components before normalization. Attribute → Token: CSPO normalizes one reward per functional attribute class across rollouts and routes its advantage to that class’s code tokens. Algorithm 1 Construct page-to-token coeficients   
Input: Raw page-reward dimensions and availability masks, reference outcomes, token supports, masks, and   
<sub>rou</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s</sub>   
Input: Switches $U , S ;$ <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s</sub> $\omega , \lambda ;$ di<sub>s ers</sub>i<sub>on</sub> t<sub>o</sub>l<sub>erance �</sub>   
Output: Token advantages � for the sampled responses   
1: for each page’s rollout group do   
2: Cli<sub>p</sub> raw <sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>e-reward dimensions to [0, 1] and com<sub>p</sub>ute masked means �<sub>�</sub> (A<sub>pp</sub>endix B.1)   
3: N<sub>orma</sub>li<sub>ze</sub> $( r _ { i } ) _ { i }$ <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e e</sub>li<sub>g</sub>ibl<sub>e same-promp</sub>t <sub>group</sub> t<sub>o o</sub>bt<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> $A _ { i } ^ { \mathrm { P } }$   
4: I<sub>n</sub>iti<sub>a</sub>li<sub>ze</sub> l<sub>oca</sub>l <sub>con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> $C _ { i , t } \gets 0$   
5: for each reference � with nonempty comparison set $S _ { k }$ do   
6: $Q _ { i , k } \gets s _ { i , k }$ if <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>, o</sub>th<sub>erw</sub>i<sub>se</sub> 0<sub>,</sub> f<sub>or</sub> $i \in S _ { k }$   
7: $( \mu _ { k } , \sigma _ { k } ) \gets$ <sub>mean an</sub>d <sub>popu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d d<sub>ev</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f $( Q _ { i , k } ) _ { i \in S _ { k } }$   
8: if $\sigma _ { k } > \epsilon$ then   
9: $a _ { i , k } \gets ( Q _ { i , k } - \mu _ { k } ) / \sigma _ { k }$ f<sub>or</sub> $i \in S _ { k }$   
10: else   
11: $a _ { i , k } \gets - U \lambda$ max $\left( 0 , 1 - \mu _ { k } \right)$ f<sub>or</sub> $i \in S _ { k }$   
12: end if   
13: for each $i \in S _ { k }$ do   
14: if $k \in \mathcal { K } _ { i }$ then   
15: $d \gets \mathsf { S C R } ( ( i , k ) , a _ { i , k } , S )$ <sub>⊲</sub> Al<sub>g</sub>orithm 2   
16: $C _ { i , t } \gets C _ { i , t } + a _ { i , k } d _ { t }$ f<sub>or</sub> $t \in \mathcal { B } _ { i , k }$   
17: else   
18: $C _ { i , t } \gets C _ { i , t } + a _ { i , k } \nu _ { i , k } ( t )$ f<sub>or response</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens</sub> �   
19: end if   
20: end for   
21: end for   
22: $A _ { i , t } \gets ( 1 - \omega ) A _ { i } ^ { \mathrm { P } } + \omega C _ { i , \cdot }$ <sub>�</sub> f<sub>or</sub> <sub>response</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens</sub> �   
23: end for

## B.2.3. Policy objective and update

The <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub> u<sub>p</sub>date uses the cli<sub>pp</sub>ed PPO surro<sub>g</sub>ate (Schulman et al., 2017). For sam<sub>p</sub>led tokens, the im<sub>p</sub>ortance <sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>o</sub> i<sub>s</sub> $\rho _ { i , t } ( \theta ) = \pi _ { \theta } ( y _ { i , t } \mid x , p , y _ { i , < t } ) / \pi _ { \theta _ { \mathrm { o l d } } } ( y _ { i , t } \mid x , p , y _ { i , < t } )$ <sub>.</sub> With <sub>c</sub>li<sub>pp</sub>i<sub>ng w</sub>idth <sub>� an</sub>d d<sub>ua</sub>l<sub>-c</sub>li<sub>p</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>or</sub> $\gamma ,$ d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>

$$
f ( \rho , A ) = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ \rho A , \mathrm { c l i p } ( \rho , 1 - \varepsilon , 1 + \varepsilon ) A \} ,
$$

$$
f _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ( \rho , A ) = \left\{ { \begin{array} { l l } { \operatorname* { m a x } \{ f ( \rho , A ) , \gamma A \} , } & { A < 0 , } \\ { f ( \rho , A ) , } & { A \geq 0 . } \end{array} } \right.\tag{7}
$$

For minibatch D and res<sub>p</sub>onse-token mask $m _ { i , t ; }$ , the objective averages over active response tokens:

$$
\widehat { \mathcal { T } } ( \theta ) = \frac { 1 } { N _ { \mathrm { a c t } } } \sum _ { i \in \mathcal { D } } \sum _ { t = 1 } ^ { T _ { i } } m _ { i , t } f _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } \Big ( \rho _ { i , t } ( \theta ) , A _ { i , t } ^ { \mathrm { U S } } \Big ) , \qquad N _ { \mathrm { a c t } } = \sum _ { i \in \mathcal { D } } \sum _ { t = 1 } ^ { T _ { i } } m _ { i , t } .\tag{8}
$$

Advantages and old-policy probabilities remain fixed while diferentiating this objective. Component variants <sub>use</sub> th<sub>e same</sub> l<sub>oss w</sub>ith th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r correspon</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en coe</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s;</sub> T<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> 6 <sub>g</sub>i<sub>ves</sub> th<sub>e op</sub>ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on se</sub>tti<sub>ngs.</sub>

## C.1.1. Training data construction and selection

Source data and serialization. We constructed the training corpus by converting the original Infinity-Doc2- 5M Document\_Parsing/doc2json annotat<sup>i</sup>ons <sup>i</sup>nto <sup>f</sup>u<sup>ll</sup>-page Mar<sup>kd</sup>own (Huang et a<sup>l</sup>., 202<sup>6</sup>). Equat<sup>i</sup>ons are written in LAT<sub>E</sub>X<sub>,</sub> and tables are written in HTML. A<sub>pp</sub>endix C.1.2 <sub>p</sub>rovides the shared SFT and RL <sub>p</sub>arsin<sub>g</sub> p<sup>r</sup>o<sup>m</sup>p<sup>t</sup>.

SFT data selection. We randomly sampled 700K and 50K page-level records from the transformed corpus as <sub>su</sub>b<sub>se</sub>t<sub>s</sub> f<sub>or superv</sub>i<sub>se</sub>d fi<sub>ne-</sub>t<sub>un</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub> Th<sub>ese su</sub>b<sub>se</sub>t<sub>s prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> SFT i<sub>n</sub>iti<sub>a</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on se</sub>tti<sub>ngs repor</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> T<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>es</sub> 1 and 4.

RL data selection. ## C.1.4. Comparison methods

The evaluated res<sub>p</sub>onse-level baselines are GRPO<sub>,</sub> GSPO<sub>,</sub> GDPO<sub>,</sub> and RDPO<sub>;</sub> P2BPO<sub>,</sub> +UAP<sub>,</sub> and +SCR are com<sub>p</sub>onent controls (Table 1).

Response-level baselines. GRPO (Shao et al., 2024) standardizes scalar page rewards within each prompt’s rollout group and broadcasts the resulting advantage across each response. GSPO (Zheng et al., 2025) retains <sub>group-re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> <sub>response</sub> <sub>a</sub>d<sub>van</sub>t<sub>ages</sub> <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> <sub>us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> l<sub>eng</sub>th<sub>-norma</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d <sub>sequence</sub> i<sub>mpor</sub>t<sub>ance</sub> <sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>os</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>sequence-</sub> level clipping. GDPO (Liu et al., 2026b) normalizes each reward dimension separately within the rollout group, aggregates the normalized dimensions, and applies batch-wise advantage normalization. RDPO (Bai et al., 2026) a<sub>pp</sub>lies ma<sub>g</sub>nitude-aware <sub>q</sub>uantile normalization followed b<sub>y</sub> Mahalanobis whitenin<sub>g</sub> within active <sub>rewar</sub>d <sub>su</sub>b<sub>spaces</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ore aggrega</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> E<sub>ac</sub>h <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>f<sub>ace pro</sub>d<sub>uces one a</sub>d<sub>van</sub>t<sub>age per samp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d <sub>response.</sub>

## C.2.1. Internal UAP interventions

Ablation protocol. The UAP ablations in Table 2 follow the primary configuration in Table 7, using full P2TPO’s fi<sub>ve pa</sub>i<sub>re</sub>d <sub>see</sub>d<sub>s an</sub>d k<sub>eep</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e m</sub>i<sub>x</sub>i<sub>ng we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht <sub>�</sub> fi<sub>xe</sub>d<sub>.</sub>

• P2TPO w/o consensus feedback (� = 0). Setting � = 0 removes the advantage assigned at imperfect reward <sub>consensus.</sub> O<sub>u</sub>t<sub>come comp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>ti<sub>on, re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve norma</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> f<sub>or non-consensus groups, om</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>on rou</sub>ti<sub>ng, an</sub>d SCR are retained.

• P2TPO w/o omission routing. Missing reference elements retain zero-valued outcomes in the group <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>cs, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>a</sub>d<sub>van</sub>t<sub>a e con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>ons are om</sub>itt<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>rom</sub> E <sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> 4<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e remove</sub>d <sub>con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>ons are no</sub>t <sub>re</sub>di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d<sub>; consensus</sub> f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>an</sub>d SCR <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>n ena</sub>bl<sub>e</sub>d<sub>.</sub>

## D.1. What is lost in aggregation?

C<sub>ons</sub>id<sub>er</sub> $G \geq 2$ <sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>a</sub>ll � <sub>re</sub>f<sub>erence</sub> bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>s are ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>, an</sub>d l<sub>e</sub>t $\mathbf { Q } \in [ 0 , 1 ] ^ { G \times K }$ <sub>con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r scores.</sub> P<sub>ar</sub>titi<sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> � <sub>nonemp</sub>t<sub>y e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t <sub>groups</sub> $\mathcal { T } _ { c } ,$ <sub>, w</sub>ith $1 < C < K$ . Here a <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> can contain several bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>s o</sub>f th<sub>e same e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t t<sub>ype;</sub> it i<sub>s coarser</sub> th<sub>an an</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>v</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l <sub>re</sub>f<sub>erence e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>.</sub> L<sub>e</sub>t $M _ { k , c } > 0$ f<sub>or</sub> $k \in \mathcal { T } _ { c }$ <sub>an</sub>d $M _ { k , c } = 0$ <sub>o</sub>th<sub>erw</sub>i<sub>se, w</sub>ith $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { k \in \mathcal { T } _ { c } } M _ { k , c } = 1 } \end{array}$ . Let $\alpha _ { c } > 0$ <sub>an</sub>d $\textstyle \sum _ { c } \alpha _ { c } = 1$ . Group scores are R = QM and the additive page utility is r = R�. Define $\mathbf { H } = \mathbf { I } _ { G } - G ^ { - 1 } \mathbf { 1 } _ { G } \mathbf { 1 } _ { G } ^ { \top }$ and the unstandardized block contrasts B = HQ.

Proposition D.1 (Strict hierarchy of aggregation losses). Block contrasts determine group contrasts HR = BM, which determine page contrasts Hr = BM�. Each reverse determination is impossible in general. More precisely, on the space of centered matrices $\mathcal { B } = \{ \mathbf { B } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { G \times K } : \mathbf { H } \mathbf { B } = \mathbf { B } \}$ ,

$$
\{ \mathbf { 0 } \} \ \subset \ \{ \mathbf { B } \in { \mathcal { B } } : \mathbf { B M } = \mathbf { 0 } \} \ \subsetneq \ \{ \mathbf { B } \in { \mathcal { B } } : \mathbf { B M } \alpha = \mathbf { 0 } \} .\tag{11}
$$

Both strict losses occur for scores in [0, 1]. Consequently, deterministic processing ofpooled rewards alone cannot recover all distinctions available at the preceding resolution.

Proof. The contrast identities follow by linearity. For the first strict inclusion, choose two blocks $k , \ell$ i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> sa<sup>m</sup>e g<sup>r</sup>oup $c ,$ and a vector x with $x _ { k } = M _ { \ell , c } , x _ { \ell } = - M _ { k , c }$ <sub>, an</sub>d <sub>a</sub>ll <sub>o</sub>th<sub>er en</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>es zero.</sub> Th<sub>en</sub> ${ \bf x } \ne 0$ b<sub>u</sub>t $\mathbf { x } ^ { \top } \mathbf { M } = 0$ F<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e secon</sub>d<sub>, c</sub>h<sub>oose</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> di<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>nc</sub>t <sub>groups �,</sub> � <sub>an</sub>d <sub>a vec</sub>t<sub>or</sub> $\mathbf { v } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { C }$ <sub>w</sub>ith $\nu _ { c } = \alpha _ { d } , \nu _ { d } = - \alpha _ { c } $ <sub>, an</sub>d <sub>o</sub>th<sub>er en</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>es</sub> zero. Thi<sub>s</sub> hi<sub>erarc</sub>h<sub>y concerns scores</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ore s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>di<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> S<sub>epara</sub>t<sub>e</sub> bl<sub>oc</sub>k <sub>norma</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on re</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns w</sub>ithi<sub>n-</sub>bl<sub>oc</sub>k <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ras</sub>t<sub>s w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> di<sub>spers</sub>i<sub>on excee</sub>d<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub>l<sub>erance,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>preserve</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l <sub>sca</sub>l<sub>es.</sub> N<sub>o</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>or</sub>d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>s asser</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>a</sub>ft<sub>er</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>di<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> Th<sub>e resu</sub>lt <sub>app</sub>li<sub>es</sub> t<sub>o a page rewar</sub>d <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> it h<sub>as</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>a</sub>dditi<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>orm;</sub> it d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>assume</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>every eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c or</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng rewar</sub>d h<sub>as</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> f<sub>orm.</sub>

Auxiliary routing lemma. Fix one rollout with disjoint nonempty block supports of lengths $L _ { k } ,$ <sub>, an</sub>d l<sub>e</sub>t $b _ { k }$ b<sub>e</sub> it<sub>s</sub> <sub>norma</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d P2BPO <sub>coe</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> b<sub>roa</sub>d<sub>cas</sub>t t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en</sub> <sub>vec</sub>t<sub>or</sub> <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>un</sub>i<sub>on</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>ese</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>-</sub>bl<sub>oc</sub>k <sub>suppor</sub>t<sub>s,</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ore page</sub>/l<sub>oca</sub>l <sub>compos</sub>iti<sub>on,</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e recons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>.</sub> L<sub>e</sub>t $D _ { \mathrm { B } } , D _ { \mathrm { E } }$ <sub>, an</sub>d $D _ { \mathrm { P } }$ b<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e m</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>mum square</sub>d t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en</sub> <sub>errors us</sub>i<sub>ng unres</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>coe</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s per</sub> bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>, per e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t <sub>group, an</sub>d <sub>per page, respec</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y.</sub> Th<sub>us</sub>

$$
D _ { \mathrm { E } } = \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \gamma } \sum _ { c } \sum _ { k \in \mathcal { I } _ { c } } L _ { k } ( b _ { k } - \gamma _ { c } ) ^ { 2 } , \qquad D _ { \mathrm { P } } = \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \nu } \sum _ { k } L _ { k } ( b _ { k } - \nu ) ^ { 2 } .
$$

Write $\begin{array} { r } { N _ { c } = \sum _ { k \in \mathcal { T } _ { c } } L _ { k } , \bar { b } _ { c } = N _ { c } ^ { - 1 } \sum _ { k \in \mathcal { T } _ { c } } L _ { k } b _ { k } , \mathrm { a n d } \bar { b } = ( \sum _ { k } L _ { k } ) ^ { - 1 } \sum _ { k } L _ { k } b _ { k } . } \end{array}$

Lemma D.2 (Resolution of block, element-group, and page routing). The optimal reconstruction errors satisfy

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { D _ { \mathrm { B } } = 0 , \qquad D _ { \mathrm { E } } = \displaystyle \sum _ { c } \sum _ { k \in \mathcal { T } _ { c } } L _ { k } ( b _ { k } - \bar { b } _ { c } ) ^ { 2 } , } } \\ { { \qquad D _ { \mathrm { P } } = D _ { \mathrm { E } } + \displaystyle \sum _ { c } N _ { c } ( \bar { b } _ { c } - \bar { b } ) ^ { 2 } . } } \end{array}\tag{12}
$$

Hence $D _ { \mathsf { B } } \leq D _ { \mathsf { E } } \leq D _ { \mathsf { P } }$ . The first inequality is strict exactly when some group contains unequal block coeficients; the second is strict exactly when the group means are not all equal.

Proof. Independent block coeficients reproduce the target. Minimizing each group sum gives $\gamma _ { c } = \bar { b } _ { c }$ <sub>, an</sub>d <sub>m</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>m</sub>i<sub>z</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e page sum g</sub>i<sub>ves</sub> $\nu = \bar { b } .$ . Ex<sub>p</sub>andin<sub>g</sub> $b _ { k } - { \bar { b } } = ( b _ { k } - { \bar { b } } _ { c } ) + ( { \bar { b } } _ { c } - { \bar { b } } )$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>e cross</sub> t<sub>erms van</sub>i<sub>s</sub>h b<sub>ecause</sub> $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { k \in \mathcal { T } _ { c } } L _ { k } \big ( b _ { k } - \bar { b } _ { c } \big ) = 0 } \end{array}$ <sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ecompos</sub>iti<sub>on an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>t<sub>ness con</sub>diti<sub>ons</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub> f<sub>rom pos</sub>iti<sub>v</sub>it<sub>y o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> l<sub>eng</sub>th<sub>s.</sub> □

Both inequalities can be strict for actual P2BPO coeficients. Proposition D.3 (Feedback activation and delivery by UAP). Let $\lambda > 0$ and $\epsilon \geq 0$ . UAP has the following properties.

1. $I f 0 < m < n$ matched rollouts all have score $s > 0$ and the other $n - m$ outcomes are missing, then $\mu = m s / n$ and $\sigma = s \sqrt { m ( n - m ) } / n$ . When $\sigma > \epsilon ,$

$$
A _ { \mathrm { m a t c h e d } } ^ { \mathrm { U } } = { \sqrt { \frac { n - m } { m } } } , \qquad A _ { \mathrm { m i s s i n g } } ^ { \mathrm { U } } = - { \sqrt { \frac { m } { n - m } } } .\tag{13}
$$

Matched-only centering instead gives zero feedback to all matched rollouts and no coeficient for omissions.

2. Whenever $\sigma \leq \epsilon ,$ every valid rollout receives $A _ { i } ^ { \mathrm { U } } = - \lambda ( c - \mu )$ . This coeficient is negative exactly when $\mu < c$ and vanishes at ceiling quality. In particular, an imperfect tie at $r < c r e c e i { \nu } e s - \lambda ( c - r )$ and an all-missing group receives −��.

3. A missing outcome with coeficient � and routing weights $\begin{array} { r } { \nu _ { t } \geq 0 , \sum _ { t } \nu _ { t } = 1 } \end{array}$ , contributes $a \nu _ { t }$ at generated tokens, preserving its signed and absolute mass:

$$
\sum _ { t } a \nu _ { t } = a , \qquad \sum _ { t } | a \nu _ { t } | = | a | .\tag{14}
$$

Proof. For the mixed group, direct evaluation gives $\sigma ^ { 2 } = m ( n - m ) s ^ { 2 } / n ^ { 2 }$ <sub>.</sub> S<sub>u</sub>b<sub>s</sub>tit<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f th<sub>e ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>score � an</sub>d <sub>m</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>score</sub> <sub>zero</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> $( Q _ { i } - \mu ) / c$ <sub>y</sub>ields E<sub>q</sub>. (13); the matched-onl<sub>y</sub> scores are constant. Proposition D.4 (Validity and target allocation of SCR). Algorithm 2’s cap-and-rebate operation yields

$$
d _ { t } = 1 - \eta + \frac { \eta } { p } { \bf 1 } [ t \in S ] , \qquad \eta = \operatorname * { m i n } \{ \beta , p ( D - 1 + \beta ) \} \in [ 0 , \beta ] .\tag{15}
$$

The weights are nonnegative and preserve the signed and absolute source-block budgets:

$$
\sum _ { t = 1 } ^ { L } A d _ { t } = L A , \qquad \sum _ { t = 1 } ^ { L } | A d _ { t } | = L | A | .\tag{16}
$$

The target’s share of absolute coeficient mass satisfies

$$
F _ { \mathcal { E } } = \frac { \sum _ { t \in \mathcal { E } } \left| A d _ { t } \right| } { L | A | } = q + \eta ( \varrho - q ) .\tag{17}
$$

It exceeds uniform allocation exactly when $\eta > 0$ and $\varrho > q .$

Proof. Before capping, selected and unselected weights are $1 - \beta + \beta / p$ <sub>an</sub>d $1 - \beta$ . Since $D > 1 ,$ <sub>, on</sub>l<sub>y se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d weights can be capped. Set � = min $\{ 1 - \beta + \beta / p , D \}$ <sub>an</sub>d $b = 1 - \beta$ <sub>.</sub> Addi<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e un</sub>if<sub>orm re</sub>b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> $1 - p a - ( 1 - p ) b$ <sub>g</sub>i<sub>ves se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht $1 + ( 1 - p ) ( a - b )$ <sub>an</sub>d <sub>unse</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht $1 - p ( a - b )$ . With <sub>�</sub> = <sub>�</sub>(�−�) = min $\{ \beta , p ( D - 1 + \beta ) \}$ these e<sub>q</sub>ual E<sub>q</sub>. (15). Because $0 \leq \eta \leq \beta \leq 1$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>nonnega</sub>ti<sub>ve,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> <sub>sum</sub> i<sub>s</sub> $L ( 1 - \eta ) + | S | \eta / p = L$ Multiplication by � or |�| proves conservation. Finally,

$$
\frac { 1 } { L } \sum _ { t \in \mathcal { E } } d _ { t } = q ( 1 - \eta ) + \frac { | S \cap \mathcal { E } | } { L } \frac { \eta } { p } = q + \eta ( \varrho - q ) .
$$

The mean period of ventilation post transplant was 302.7 (SD 617.8), median 102 h (IQR298), On Spearman's rank correl ation, this was not associated with the donor/recipient weight ratio (Fig 3) (p = -0.12, p = 0.14). Even when excluding the 11 patients who had been intubated for more than 100 h, there was no association (p = -0.10, p = 0.38) [Fig. 4]. The mean period of ITU stay was 448 h (SD 749.2), median 184 h (IQR374). On Spearman's rank correlation, this was also not related to the weight ratio (p = -0.04, p = 0.58) (Fig. 5). Azain. if the six patients who were in the ITU for more than 2000 h were excluded, there was sill no association (p < 0.01, p = 0.98).

## E. Qualitative Case Studies

Of the 104 patients who had received an ambulatory blood pressure anahysis during their follow-up period, 25 (18.4%) were hypertensive and receiving treatment for it. On Kaplan-Meier analysis of freedom from hypertension from the time of hospita discharge to date of last ambulatory blood-pressure measure ment, there was no difference between the three weight groups (log-rankp = 0.39) (Fig. 3).

## Secondary outcome measure

161 patients. Thirty-eight patients (24%) developed RV failure. In the lower tertile group, 18.9% developed postoperative Ry failure, in the middle group 24.5% and in the upper tertile group 28.8%; this was not statistically different (p = 0.48) (Table 3).

## Extreme mismatch subgroup

There were nine patients in whom the donor-recipient weight mismatch was 3 or greater (5.5%). By the end of the follow-up period, there was only 11% (1/9) mortality, compared with 18.2% (23/126) for those with a ratio of <3 (p= 0.76). ## Monomer rynthesis

BPAM was synthesized from the reaction of 4-butylaniline with acryloyl chloride, with triethylamine as the acid receptor, using the method described by MeCormick et al. [6]. ## Poljmer sywthesis

A micellar polymerization technique was used to prepare poly mers using sdum dodecy sulfate (SDS) as the surfactant and potassium persulfate as the free-radical initiator [6]. The appropriate amount of ionizable monomer (AA) was dissolved in deionized water and the pH was adjusted to 9–10 with NaOH to form the water-soluble sodium salt (sodium acrylate), AM. BPAM. and SD e added respctiely and sed uder until a clear solution was observed. The solution was then heated to 50 C, and the initiator was added. The polymerization wus conducted for 12 h. The polsmer mistures were diluted with water and precipitated into acetone and then washed with acetone. The polymer was dried under roduced pressure at 40 C o 4 h. ## Measurements

Elemental analysis was performed with a Elementar-CHNO-Rapio to determine the carbon, nitrogen, and hydrogen content. The Fourier transform IR measurement was condacted psing a Biorad/ Digah FTS40 and the H NM measuements were carried out on the BPAM solution in CDCl using a Bruker Ac250. UV spectra were obtained with a Spekäralnhotometer DMR 10. Pyrene fuorescence studies were performed with a Shimadzu RF-S301 PC spectrouorophoometer (x=335 nm I =373 nm, I2 = 384 nm). Viscosity measurements and oscillatory rheological measurements were conducted with a Bohlin CS rheometer with a cone/plate or double-gap concentric cylinder measuring geometry with a cons anele of 4° and a dameter of 40 mm. The doubie-gan device is applicabke for loa-viscosity liquids The zero-shear viscovty i délute solution was measured with a Paar OCR-D oscillating canillary rheometer, The measurgment temperature was 25 C, and shear rate was é s-1 unless otherwise noted. The atom force electron controller with a Dimension 310 microscope, and all measurements were performed in tapping mode.
